People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

It’s not ruining everything, but it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and are not about making mistakes or checking things?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed. The repeating thoughts are mostly tied to worrying I made an error or missed something. I can’t think of a separate kind of thought that keeps looping on its own.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these repetitive thoughts and checking behaviors cause you?\n\nPatient: It causes me a fair amount of distress. Even when I’m trying to focus on a system design or prepare a lesson, the thought barges in that maybe I sent the wrong message to my ex-wife, or mishandled some family photos, or let someone in the community down.\n\nI know I’ve already gone over it, but I can’t seem to just let it pass. It gets me tense and distracted, and I feel like I have to check or replay it again before I can settle down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or activities because of your fear of making mistakes or your need to check things repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I don’t always avoid things completely, but I’ll put them off because I know I’m going to get stuck checking.\n\nLike before I leave the house or start work, I can lose a lot of time re-reading emails and texts, reopening folders, and replaying conversations in my head. It’s like I’m trying to prove to myself I didn’t make some serious mistake. So sometimes I delay starting something, or I avoid opening a message or folder because I’m worried I’ll get pulled into that loop again.\n\nDoctor: Have your repetitive checking behaviors or recurring thoughts affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. Mostly it shows up in how I’m not as reliable as I used to be. My work has slowed down, and I’ve missed design milestones because I get caught up checking things.\n\nI’ve also had to postpone mentoring sessions I normally would handle without a problem. And there was a youth coding workshop that mattered to me where I was late and not really prepared. I felt pretty embarrassed about that. So yes, it’s affected people who count on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your personal relationships, such as with your ex-wife or friends, due to your checking behaviors or recurring thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, the clearest impact has been that people can’t count on me the same way. My work has slowed down enough that I’ve missed design milestones, and I’ve had to postpone mentoring sessions I used to handle pretty reliably.\n\nI also felt really embarrassed when I was late and not prepared for a youth coding workshop that mattered to me. I’m trying to feel sure I didn’t make some serious mistake, but it can eat up a lot of time.\n\nDoctor: Has this repetitive checking and rehearsing of conversations and actions interfered with your work, relationships, or daily life in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. At work, I’m slower than I used to be. I’ve missed some design milestones because I got stuck rechecking things instead of moving forward. I’ve also had to postpone mentoring sessions that I used to handle pretty reliably.\n\nAnd outside of work, there was a youth coding workshop that mattered to me, and I showed up late and not really prepared. I felt embarrassed about that. It’s not ruining everything, but it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and are not about making mistakes or checking things?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed. The repeating thoughts are mostly tied to worrying I made an error or missed something. I can’t think of a separate kind of thought that keeps looping on its own.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these repetitive thoughts and checking behaviors cause you?\n\nPatient: It causes me a fair amount of distress. Even when I’m trying to focus on a system design or prepare a lesson, the thought barges in that maybe I sent the wrong message to my ex-wife, or mishandled some family photos, or let someone in the community down.\n\nI know I’ve already gone over it, but I can’t seem to just let it pass. It gets me tense and distracted, and I feel like I have to check or replay it again before I can settle down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or activities because of your fear of making mistakes or your need to check things repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I don’t always avoid things completely, but I’ll put them off because I know I’m going to get stuck checking.\n\nLike before I leave the house or start work, I can lose a lot of time re-reading emails and texts, reopening folders, and replaying conversations in my head. It’s like I’m trying to prove to myself I didn’t make some serious mistake. So sometimes I delay starting something, or I avoid opening a message or folder because I’m worried I’ll get pulled into that loop again.\n\nDoctor: Have your repetitive checking behaviors or recurring thoughts affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. Mostly it shows up in how I’m not as reliable as I used to be. My work has slowed down, and I’ve missed design milestones because I get caught up checking things.\n\nI’ve also had to postpone mentoring sessions I normally would handle without a problem. And there was a youth coding workshop that mattered to me where I was late and not really prepared. I felt pretty embarrassed about that. So yes, it’s affected people who count on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your personal relationships, such as with your ex-wife or friends, due to your checking behaviors or recurring thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, the clearest impact has been that people can’t count on me the same way. My work has slowed down enough that I’ve missed design milestones, and I’ve had to postpone mentoring sessions I used to handle pretty reliably.\n\nI also felt really embarrassed when I was late and not prepared for a youth coding workshop that mattered to me. So, yes, it has caused problems in my relationships and responsibilities with people around me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life where you feel like your checking behaviors or recurring thoughts are causing problems or making it hard for you to function?\n\nPatient: Yes. The main area is work and commitments connected to work. I’m slower than I should be because I keep checking, and it’s gotten to the point where I’ve missed design milestones.\n\nI’ve also had to postpone mentoring sessions that I used to handle without much trouble. And there was a youth coding workshop I cared about where I showed up late and wasn’t prepared. That was embarrassing, and it made me feel like I’m not functioning the way I should be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I keep losing a lot of time checking things over and over. I’ll re-read emails or texts, reopen folders, and replay conversations in my head before I leave the house or start work. It’s like I’m trying to convince myself I didn’t make some serious mistake, but I can’t seem to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're checking things over and over?\n\nPatient: Yes. When I’m re-reading emails or texts, or reopening folders, it’s usually because I’m worried I’ve missed something or made a serious mistake. I’ll keep going back over it trying to reassure myself, but it doesn’t really settle, and I end up losing a lot of time.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s usually the same kind of thought that barges in when I’m trying to focus, like when I’m working through a system design or preparing a lesson. I’ll suddenly think maybe I sent the wrong message to my ex-wife, or mishandled some family photos, or let someone in the community down somehow. Even if I know I already checked or thought it through, I can’t seem to just let it pass.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way or a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Not really a certain number of times, no. It’s more that I keep doing it until it feels like I’ve proved to myself I didn’t make a serious mistake. So I’ll re-read the email or text again, reopen the folder again, or replay the conversation in my head before I can leave or start working. Sometimes it takes a lot longer than I mean for it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do certain physical actions, like touching or tapping, in order to relieve your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Not really touching or tapping, no. For me it’s more checking things—re-reading emails and texts, reopening folders, and going over conversations in my head before I leave the house or start work. I’m trying to feel sure I didn’t make some serious mistake, but it can eat up a lot of time.\n\nDoctor: Has this repetitive checking and rehearsing of conversations and actions interfered with your work, relationships, or daily life in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. At work, I’m slower than I used to be. I’ve missed some design milestones because I got stuck rechecking things instead of moving forward. I’ve also had to postpone mentoring sessions that I used to handle pretty reliably.\n\nAnd outside of work, there was a youth coding workshop that mattered to me, and I showed up late and not really prepared. I felt embarrassed about that. It’s not ruining everything, but it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and are not about making mistakes or checking things?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed. The repeating thoughts are mostly tied to worrying I made an error or missed something. I can’t think of a separate kind of thought that keeps looping on its own.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these repetitive thoughts and checking behaviors cause you?\n\nPatient: It causes me a fair amount of distress. Even when I’m trying to focus on a system design or prepare a lesson, the thought barges in that maybe I sent the wrong message to my ex-wife, or mishandled some family photos, or let someone in the community down.\n\nI know I’ve already gone over it, but I can’t seem to just let it pass. It gets me tense and distracted, and I feel like I have to check or replay it again before I can settle down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or activities because of your fear of making mistakes or your need to check things repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I don’t always avoid things completely, but I’ll put them off because I know I’m going to get stuck checking.\n\nLike before I leave the house or start work, I can lose a lot of time re-reading emails and texts, reopening folders, and replaying conversations in my head. It’s like I’m trying to prove to myself I didn’t make some serious mistake. So sometimes I delay starting something, or I avoid opening a message or folder because I’m worried I’ll get pulled into that loop again.\n\nDoctor: Have your repetitive checking behaviors or recurring thoughts affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. Mostly it shows up in how I’m not as reliable as I used to be. My work has slowed down, and I’ve missed design milestones because I get caught up checking things.\n\nI’ve also had to postpone mentoring sessions I normally would handle without a problem. And there was a youth coding workshop that mattered to me where I was late and not really prepared. I felt pretty embarrassed about that. So yes, it’s affected people who count on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your personal relationships, such as with your ex-wife or friends, due to your checking behaviors or recurring thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, the clearest impact has been that people can’t count on me the same way. My work has slowed down enough that I’ve missed design milestones, and I’ve had to postpone mentoring sessions I used to handle pretty reliably.\n\nI also felt really embarrassed when I was late and not prepared for a youth coding workshop that mattered to me. So, yes, it has caused problems in my relationships and responsibilities with people around me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life where you feel like your checking behaviors or recurring thoughts are causing problems or making it hard for you to function?\n\nPatient: Yes. The main area is work and commitments connected to work. I’m slower than I should be because I keep checking, and it’s gotten to the point where I’ve missed design milestones.\n\nI’ve also had to postpone mentoring sessions that I used to handle without much trouble. And there was a youth coding workshop I cared about where I showed up late and wasn’t prepared. That was embarrassing, and it made me feel like I’m not functioning the way I should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge even when you're not checking things or thinking about potential mistakes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t paid close attention to whether I feel anxious when I’m not checking or worrying about mistakes. It mostly stands out to me when I’m caught up in the checking itself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about potential mistakes or checking things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really noticed anxiety outside of the checking or worrying about mistakes. It’s possible I am a bit tense, but it’s not something I’ve been tracking clearly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It mostly stands out to me when I’m caught up in the checking itself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about potential mistakes or checking things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really noticed anxiety outside of the checking or worrying about mistakes. It’s possible I am a bit tense, but it’s not something I’ve been tracking clearly.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel anxious when you're doing things you enjoy and not thinking about potential mistakes?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 56 / computer_occupation / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

I don’t really know. When I’m doing something I enjoy, I’m usually focused on that, so I haven’t noticed being anxious in those moments. I guess I’d have to pay more attention to it.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I keep losing a lot of time checking things over and over. I’ll re-read emails or texts, reopen folders, and replay conversations in my head before I leave the house or start work. It’s like I’m trying to convince myself I didn’t make some serious mistake, but I can’t seem to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're checking things over and over?\n\nPatient: Yes. When I’m re-reading emails or texts, or reopening folders, it’s usually because I’m worried I’ve missed something or made a serious mistake. I’ll keep going back over it trying to reassure myself, but it doesn’t really settle, and I end up losing a lot of time.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s usually the same kind of thought that barges in when I’m trying to focus, like when I’m working through a system design or preparing a lesson. I’ll suddenly think maybe I sent the wrong message to my ex-wife, or mishandled some family photos, or let someone in the community down somehow. Even if I know I already checked or thought it through, I can’t seem to just let it pass.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way or a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Not really a certain number of times, no. It’s more that I keep doing it until it feels like I’ve proved to myself I didn’t make a serious mistake. So I’ll re-read the email or text again, reopen the folder again, or replay the conversation in my head before I can leave or start working. Sometimes it takes a lot longer than I mean for it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do certain physical actions, like touching or tapping, in order to relieve your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Not really touching or tapping, no. For me it’s more checking things—re-reading emails and texts, reopening folders, and going over conversations in my head before I leave the house or start work. I’m trying to feel sure I didn’t make some serious mistake, but it can eat up a lot of time.\n\nDoctor: Has this repetitive checking and rehearsing of conversations and actions interfered with your work, relationships, or daily life in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. At work, I’m slower than I used to be. I’ve missed some design milestones because I got stuck rechecking things instead of moving forward. I’ve also had to postpone mentoring sessions that I used to handle pretty reliably.\n\nAnd outside of work, there was a youth coding workshop that mattered to me, and I showed up late and not really prepared. I felt embarrassed about that. It’s not ruining everything, but it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and are not about making mistakes or checking things?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed. The repeating thoughts are mostly tied to worrying I made an error or missed something. I can’t think of a separate kind of thought that keeps looping on its own.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these repetitive thoughts and checking behaviors cause you?\n\nPatient: It causes me a fair amount of distress. Even when I’m trying to focus on a system design or prepare a lesson, the thought barges in that maybe I sent the wrong message to my ex-wife, or mishandled some family photos, or let someone in the community down.\n\nI know I’ve already gone over it, but I can’t seem to just let it pass. It gets me tense and distracted, and I feel like I have to check or replay it again before I can settle down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or activities because of your fear of making mistakes or your need to check things repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I don’t always avoid things completely, but I’ll put them off because I know I’m going to get stuck checking.\n\nLike before I leave the house or start work, I can lose a lot of time re-reading emails and texts, reopening folders, and replaying conversations in my head. It’s like I’m trying to prove to myself I didn’t make some serious mistake. So sometimes I delay starting something, or I avoid opening a message or folder because I’m worried I’ll get pulled into that loop again.\n\nDoctor: Have your repetitive checking behaviors or recurring thoughts affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. Mostly it shows up in how I’m not as reliable as I used to be. My work has slowed down, and I’ve missed design milestones because I get caught up checking things.\n\nI’ve also had to postpone mentoring sessions I normally would handle without a problem. And there was a youth coding workshop that mattered to me where I was late and not really prepared. I felt pretty embarrassed about that. So yes, it’s affected people who count on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your personal relationships, such as with your ex-wife or friends, due to your checking behaviors or recurring thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, the clearest impact has been that people can’t count on me the same way. My work has slowed down enough that I’ve missed design milestones, and I’ve had to postpone mentoring sessions I used to handle pretty reliably.\n\nI also felt really embarrassed when I was late and not prepared for a youth coding workshop that mattered to me. So, yes, it has caused problems in my relationships and responsibilities with people around me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life where you feel like your checking behaviors or recurring thoughts are causing problems or making it hard for you to function?\n\nPatient: Yes. The main area is work and commitments connected to work. I’m slower than I should be because I keep checking, and it’s gotten to the point where I’ve missed design milestones.\n\nI’ve also had to postpone mentoring sessions that I used to handle without much trouble. And there was a youth coding workshop I cared about where I showed up late and wasn’t prepared. That was embarrassing, and it made me feel like I’m not functioning the way I should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge even when you're not checking things or thinking about potential mistakes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t paid close attention to whether I feel anxious when I’m not checking or worrying about mistakes. It mostly stands out to me when I’m caught up in the checking itself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about potential mistakes or checking things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really noticed anxiety outside of the checking or worrying about mistakes. It’s possible I am a bit tense, but it’s not something I’ve been tracking clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're doing things you enjoy and not thinking about potential mistakes?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. When I’m doing something I enjoy, I’m usually focused on that, so I haven’t noticed being anxious in those moments. I guess I’d have to pay more attention to it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s not ruining everything, but it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and are not about making mistakes or checking things?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed. The repeating thoughts are mostly tied to worrying I made an error or missed something. I can’t think of a separate kind of thought that keeps looping on its own.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these repetitive thoughts and checking behaviors cause you?\n\nPatient: It causes me a fair amount of distress. Even when I’m trying to focus on a system design or prepare a lesson, the thought barges in that maybe I sent the wrong message to my ex-wife, or mishandled some family photos, or let someone in the community down.\n\nI know I’ve already gone over it, but I can’t seem to just let it pass. It gets me tense and distracted, and I feel like I have to check or replay it again before I can settle down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or activities because of your fear of making mistakes or your need to check things repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I don’t always avoid things completely, but I’ll put them off because I know I’m going to get stuck checking.\n\nLike before I leave the house or start work, I can lose a lot of time re-reading emails and texts, reopening folders, and replaying conversations in my head. It’s like I’m trying to prove to myself I didn’t make some serious mistake. So sometimes I delay starting something, or I avoid opening a message or folder because I’m worried I’ll get pulled into that loop again.\n\nDoctor: Have your repetitive checking behaviors or recurring thoughts affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. Mostly it shows up in how I’m not as reliable as I used to be. My work has slowed down, and I’ve missed design milestones because I get caught up checking things.\n\nI’ve also had to postpone mentoring sessions I normally would handle without a problem. And there was a youth coding workshop that mattered to me where I was late and not really prepared. I felt pretty embarrassed about that. So yes, it’s affected people who count on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your personal relationships, such as with your ex-wife or friends, due to your checking behaviors or recurring thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, the clearest impact has been that people can’t count on me the same way. My work has slowed down enough that I’ve missed design milestones, and I’ve had to postpone mentoring sessions I used to handle pretty reliably.\n\nI also felt really embarrassed when I was late and not prepared for a youth coding workshop that mattered to me. So, yes, it has caused problems in my relationships and responsibilities with people around me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life where you feel like your checking behaviors or recurring thoughts are causing problems or making it hard for you to function?\n\nPatient: Yes. The main area is work and commitments connected to work. I’m slower than I should be because I keep checking, and it’s gotten to the point where I’ve missed design milestones.\n\nI’ve also had to postpone mentoring sessions that I used to handle without much trouble. And there was a youth coding workshop I cared about where I showed up late and wasn’t prepared. That was embarrassing, and it made me feel like I’m not functioning the way I should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge even when you're not checking things or thinking about potential mistakes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t paid close attention to whether I feel anxious when I’m not checking or worrying about mistakes. It mostly stands out to me when I’m caught up in the checking itself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about potential mistakes or checking things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really noticed anxiety outside of the checking or worrying about mistakes. It’s possible I am a bit tense, but it’s not something I’ve been tracking clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're doing things you enjoy and not thinking about potential mistakes?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. When I’m doing something I enjoy, I’m usually focused on that, so I haven’t noticed being anxious in those moments. I guess I’d have to pay more attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're completely relaxed and not thinking about anything in particular?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. If I’m completely relaxed, I don’t really notice anxiety, or at least it doesn’t stand out to me. I haven’t paid close attention to that specific situation.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF42.2, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I keep losing a lot of time checking things over and over. I’ll re-read emails or texts, reopen folders, and replay conversations in my head before I leave the house or start work. It’s like I’m trying to convince myself I didn’t make some serious mistake, but I can’t seem to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're checking things over and over?\n\nPatient: Yes. When I’m re-reading emails or texts, or reopening folders, it’s usually because I’m worried I’ve missed something or made a serious mistake. I’ll keep going back over it trying to reassure myself, but it doesn’t really settle, and I end up losing a lot of time.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s usually the same kind of thought that barges in when I’m trying to focus, like when I’m working through a system design or preparing a lesson. I’ll suddenly think maybe I sent the wrong message to my ex-wife, or mishandled some family photos, or let someone in the community down somehow. Even if I know I already checked or thought it through, I can’t seem to just let it pass.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way or a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Not really a certain number of times, no. It’s more that I keep doing it until it feels like I’ve proved to myself I didn’t make a serious mistake. So I’ll re-read the email or text again, reopen the folder again, or replay the conversation in my head before I can leave or start working. Sometimes it takes a lot longer than I mean for it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do certain physical actions, like touching or tapping, in order to relieve your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Not really touching or tapping, no. For me it’s more checking things—re-reading emails and texts, reopening folders, and going over conversations in my head before I leave the house or start work. I’m trying to feel sure I didn’t make some serious mistake, but it can eat up a lot of time.\n\nDoctor: Has this repetitive checking and rehearsing of conversations and actions interfered with your work, relationships, or daily life in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. At work, I’m slower than I used to be. I’ve missed some design milestones because I got stuck rechecking things instead of moving forward. I’ve also had to postpone mentoring sessions that I used to handle pretty reliably.\n\nAnd outside of work, there was a youth coding workshop that mattered to me, and I showed up late and not really prepared. I felt embarrassed about that. It’s not ruining everything, but it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and are not about making mistakes or checking things?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed. The repeating thoughts are mostly tied to worrying I made an error or missed something. I can’t think of a separate kind of thought that keeps looping on its own.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these repetitive thoughts and checking behaviors cause you?\n\nPatient: It causes me a fair amount of distress. Even when I’m trying to focus on a system design or prepare a lesson, the thought barges in that maybe I sent the wrong message to my ex-wife, or mishandled some family photos, or let someone in the community down.\n\nI know I’ve already gone over it, but I can’t seem to just let it pass. It gets me tense and distracted, and I feel like I have to check or replay it again before I can settle down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or activities because of your fear of making mistakes or your need to check things repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I don’t always avoid things completely, but I’ll put them off because I know I’m going to get stuck checking.\n\nLike before I leave the house or start work, I can lose a lot of time re-reading emails and texts, reopening folders, and replaying conversations in my head. It’s like I’m trying to prove to myself I didn’t make some serious mistake. So sometimes I delay starting something, or I avoid opening a message or folder because I’m worried I’ll get pulled into that loop again.\n\nDoctor: Have your repetitive checking behaviors or recurring thoughts affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. Mostly it shows up in how I’m not as reliable as I used to be. My work has slowed down, and I’ve missed design milestones because I get caught up checking things.\n\nI’ve also had to postpone mentoring sessions I normally would handle without a problem. And there was a youth coding workshop that mattered to me where I was late and not really prepared. I felt pretty embarrassed about that. So yes, it’s affected people who count on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your personal relationships, such as with your ex-wife or friends, due to your checking behaviors or recurring thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, the clearest impact has been that people can’t count on me the same way. My work has slowed down enough that I’ve missed design milestones, and I’ve had to postpone mentoring sessions I used to handle pretty reliably.\n\nI also felt really embarrassed when I was late and not prepared for a youth coding workshop that mattered to me. So, yes, it has caused problems in my relationships and responsibilities with people around me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life where you feel like your checking behaviors or recurring thoughts are causing problems or making it hard for you to function?\n\nPatient: Yes. The main area is work and commitments connected to work. I’m slower than I should be because I keep checking, and it’s gotten to the point where I’ve missed design milestones.\n\nI’ve also had to postpone mentoring sessions that I used to handle without much trouble. And there was a youth coding workshop I cared about where I showed up late and wasn’t prepared. That was embarrassing, and it made me feel like I’m not functioning the way I should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge even when you're not checking things or thinking about potential mistakes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t paid close attention to whether I feel anxious when I’m not checking or worrying about mistakes. It mostly stands out to me when I’m caught up in the checking itself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about potential mistakes or checking things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really noticed anxiety outside of the checking or worrying about mistakes. It’s possible I am a bit tense, but it’s not something I’ve been tracking clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're doing things you enjoy and not thinking about potential mistakes?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. When I’m doing something I enjoy, I’m usually focused on that, so I haven’t noticed being anxious in those moments. I guess I’d have to pay more attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're completely relaxed and not thinking about anything in particular?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. If I’m completely relaxed, I don’t really notice anxiety, or at least it doesn’t stand out to me. I haven’t paid close attention to that specific situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are not thinking about work or any responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. If I’m not thinking about work or responsibilities, I don’t usually notice anxiety, but I haven’t paid close attention to that specifically.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"intent": "determine if anxiety is present outside of checking behaviors", "question": "Do you feel anxious when you are not thinking about work, responsibilities, or potential mistakes?"}

My work has slowed down, and I’ve missed design milestones because I get caught up checking things.\n\nI’ve also had to postpone mentoring sessions I normally would handle without a problem. And there was a youth coding workshop that mattered to me where I was late and not really prepared. I felt pretty embarrassed about that. So yes, it’s affected people who count on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your personal relationships, such as with your ex-wife or friends, due to your checking behaviors or recurring thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, the clearest impact has been that people can’t count on me the same way. My work has slowed down enough that I’ve missed design milestones, and I’ve had to postpone mentoring sessions I used to handle pretty reliably.\n\nI also felt really embarrassed when I was late and not prepared for a youth coding workshop that mattered to me. So, yes, it has caused problems in my relationships and responsibilities with people around me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life where you feel like your checking behaviors or recurring thoughts are causing problems or making it hard for you to function?\n\nPatient: Yes. The main area is work and commitments connected to work. I’m slower than I should be because I keep checking, and it’s gotten to the point where I’ve missed design milestones.\n\nI’ve also had to postpone mentoring sessions that I used to handle without much trouble. And there was a youth coding workshop I cared about where I showed up late and wasn’t prepared. That was embarrassing, and it made me feel like I’m not functioning the way I should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge even when you're not checking things or thinking about potential mistakes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t paid close attention to whether I feel anxious when I’m not checking or worrying about mistakes. It mostly stands out to me when I’m caught up in the checking itself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about potential mistakes or checking things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really noticed anxiety outside of the checking or worrying about mistakes. It’s possible I am a bit tense, but it’s not something I’ve been tracking clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're doing things you enjoy and not thinking about potential mistakes?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. When I’m doing something I enjoy, I’m usually focused on that, so I haven’t noticed being anxious in those moments. I guess I’d have to pay more attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're completely relaxed and not thinking about anything in particular?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. If I’m completely relaxed, I don’t really notice anxiety, or at least it doesn’t stand out to me. I haven’t paid close attention to that specific situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are not thinking about work or any responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. If I’m not thinking about work or responsibilities, I don’t usually notice anxiety, but I haven’t paid close attention to that specifically.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are not thinking about work, responsibilities, or potential mistakes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. If I’m not thinking about any of that, I don’t usually notice anxiety. I guess I haven’t really paid attention to whether it’s there in the background.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Even if I know I already checked or thought it through, I can’t seem to just let it pass.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way or a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Not really a certain number of times, no. It’s more that I keep doing it until it feels like I’ve proved to myself I didn’t make a serious mistake. So I’ll re-read the email or text again, reopen the folder again, or replay the conversation in my head before I can leave or start working. Sometimes it takes a lot longer than I mean for it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do certain physical actions, like touching or tapping, in order to relieve your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Not really touching or tapping, no. I’m trying to feel sure I didn’t make some serious mistake, but it can eat up a lot of time.\n\nDoctor: Has this repetitive checking and rehearsing of conversations and actions interfered with your work, relationships, or daily life in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. Even if I know I already checked or thought it through, I can’t seem to just let it pass.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way or a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Not really a certain number of times, no. It’s more that I keep doing it until it feels like I’ve proved to myself I didn’t make a serious mistake. So I’ll re-read the email or text again, reopen the folder again, or replay the conversation in my head before I can leave or start working. I’m trying to feel sure I didn’t make some serious mistake, but it can eat up a lot of time.\n\nDoctor: Has this repetitive checking and rehearsing of conversations and actions interfered with your work, relationships, or daily life in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I felt embarrassed about that. It gets me tense and distracted, and I feel like I have to check or replay it again before I can settle down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or activities because of your fear of making mistakes or your need to check things repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I don’t always avoid things completely, but I’ll put them off because I know I’m going to get stuck checking.\n\nLike before I leave the house or start work, I can lose a lot of time re-reading emails and texts, reopening folders, and replaying conversations in my head. It’s like I’m trying to prove to myself I didn’t make some serious mistake.
